PREFACE ]

This Report is prepared for submission to the Governor of the State of
Rajasthan under Article 151 of the Constitution of India.

The audit of revenue receipts of the State Government is conducted under
Section 16 of the Comptroller and Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and
Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. This Report presents the results of audit of
receipts comprising commercial taxes, taxes on vehicles, stamp duty and
registration fees, other tax and non-tax receipts of the Government of
Rajasthan.

The cases mentioned in the Report are among those which came to notice in
the course of test audit of accounts during the year 2012-13, as well as those
which had come to notice in earlier years but could not be reported in previous
Audit Reports; matters relating to the period subsequent to 2012-13 have also
been included, wherever necessary.

The audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing Standards
issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.

v


OVERVIEW

This Report contains 39 paragraphs involving ¥ 586.49 crore, including two
Performance Audits on ‘Recovery of arrears in Commercial Taxes
Department’ and ‘Encroachment on Government Land’, Some of the
signiticant audit findings are mentioned below:

[ I._General

The total revenue receipts of the Government of Rajasthan during 2012-13
were % 66,913.01 crore as against = 57,010.76 crore for the year 2011-12. The
revenue raised by the Government amounted to हैं 42,636.24 crore comprising
tax revenue of % 30,502.65 crore and non-tax revenue of € 12,133.59 crore.
The receipts from the Government of India were हैं 24,276.77 crore (State’s
share of divisible Union taxes of हैं 17,102.85 crore and grants-in-aid of
& 7,173.92 crore).

(Paragraph 1.1)

2,882 Inspection Reports (IRs) containing 9,489 paragraphs involving
₹ 7,731.42 crore remained outstanding at the end of June 2013. Of these,
first replies to 80 IRs issued upto December 2012, were not received till
30 June 2013.

(Paragraph 1.2.1)
Test check during 2012-13 revealed underassessment, non levy/short levy of
taxes and loss of revenue, failure to raise demands and other irregularities
aggregating to = 2,286.17 crore in 37,959 cases. The concemed Departments
accepted underassessments and other deficiencies of F 598.39 crore involved
in 23,519 cases, of which 11,973 cases involving % 217.42 crore were pointed
out in audit during the year 2012-13 and the rest in earlier years. The
Departments recovered हैं 73.07 crore in 5,876 cases at the instance of audit
during the year 2012-13.

(Paragraph 1.4.1)

IL. _ Taxes on Sales, Trade etc.

A Performance Audit of ‘Recovery of arrears in Commercial Taxes
Department’ revealed the following:

e ©The tax arrears increased from हैं 2,996.96 crore to हैं 4,838.04 crore
during the period 2007-08 to 2011-12.

(Paragraph 2.9.7.1)

e No time frame was prescribed in the RVAT Act, 2003 or RVAT Rules,
2006 or by the Department to ensure initiation of recovery proceedings.

(Paragraph 2.9.9,3)

e = Arrears of % 69.01 lakh in four cases were not recovered due to failure of
auctions of property.

(Paragraph 2.9.10)

vii


tudit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

e — Test check of 259 cases of ex-parte assessments pertaining to the period
1977-78 to 2009-10 revealed non-recovery of demand of हैं 49.44 crore
and interest of € 37.81 crore.

(Paragraph 2.9.12)

e Eight contractors who were engaged in collection of tax at check posts
did not deposit the arrears amounting to % 5.11 crore.

(Paragraph 2.9.13)

e Arrears of हैं 82.42 lakh against two active dealers were not recovered
though they were assessed regularly.
(Paragraph 2.9.14)
e Inadequate follow up action and delay in issuing Revenue Recovery
Certificates in 12 cases pertaining to the dealers who were having
property in other States resulted in non-recovery of € 9.09 crore.

(Paragraph 2.9.15)

© Scrutiny of 48 cases revealed that dealers claimed input tax credit of
₹ 13.00 crore but the Assessing Authorities (AAs) did not allow the same
for want of verification report from concerned AAs.

(Paragraph 2.9.16)

° Test check of recovery records of two dealers disclosed that AAs had not
lodged interest claims with the Official Liquidator. This resulted in loss of
interest of € 5.86 crore.

(Paragraph 2.9.20)

Acceptance of Central Sales Tax (CST) declaration forms after assessment in
contravention of CST Rules resulted in irregular concession/exemption of tax
of = 1.14 crore and interest of f 28.65 lakh in nine cases.

(Paragraph 2.14)

Incorrect determination of tax payable resulted in excess grant of subsidy of
₹ 1.46 crore and loss of interest of € 12.08 lakh.

(Paragraph 2.15.1 and 2.15.2)

Incorrect grant of deferment of tax resulted in short revenue realisation of
₹ 3.65 crore besides interest of f 1.09 crore.

(Paragraph 2.16.1)

Ill. Taxes on Motor Vehicles

Motor vehicle tax and special road tax of € 10.12 crore in respect of 3,705
vehicles for the period between April 2009 and March 2012 were either not paid
or paid short.

(Paragraph 3.9.1)
Lump sum tax of % 49.13 lakh payable by 117 vehicles was either not paid or
paid short resulting in short-realisation/non-realisation of revenue.

(Paragraph 3.9.2)

Overview

Land Revenue

A Performance Audit on “Encroachment on Government Land’ revealed the
following:

4,503 trespassers or their family members continued to encroach on the
same pieces of Government land year after year, the total land encroached
in 13 ¢ehsils, aggregated to 5,726.77 hectares. Out of these, 1,014
trespassers individually encroached land of five Bigha or more which
worked out to 1,805.92 hectares valuing € 61.27 crore.

(Paragraph 4.7.8.1)

In 56 cases of Mangrol Tehsil, penalty and auction value of crops
amounting to 7.09 lakh for the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12 was yet
to be recovered.
(Paragraph 4.7.9)
In 156 cases of Kotputli Tehsi/, there was no evidence of auction of
seized crop valuing € 27.79 lakh on 83.23 hectares of Government land.
(Paragraph 4.7.9)

Department realised only हैं 1.76 crore by auction of crops cultivated
illegally on 11,099.97 hectares of Government land. Realisable value of
the crop works out to = 24.46 crore on the basis of Minimum Support
Price and productivity per hectare.

(Paragraph 4.7.10)
Due to non-regularisation of the wells constructed on Government land,
revenue of € 1,68 crore could not be realised.

(Paragraph 4.7.11)
In Gangapur City Tehsil, 77.27 hectares of evacuee land valuing
& 2.12 crore was under continuous encroachment.

(Paragraph 4.7.13.2)

Allotment of 49.9 hectares of encroached land valuing % 1.34 crore was
made to 128 trespassers at reduced cost of € 21.59 lakh on the basis of
incorrect facts, causing loss to exchequer.

(Paragraph 4.7.14)
There was a loss of revenue amounting to हैं 34,93 lakh on allotment of

8.63 hectares of Government land to trespassers on concessional rates
due to application of incorrect rule in Banswara Tehsil.

(Paragraph 4.7.14)

Tehsildars levied penalties of& 1.04 lakh against trespassers who had
encroached upon valuable Government land valuing ¥ 160.66 crore for
housing and commercial purposes.

(Paragraph 4.7.15.1)



Audit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

In 209 cases of Sojat City Tehsil, 111 trespassers had encroached upon
286.57 hectare of Government land valuing = 3.82 crore since 1981-82
for growing Mehandi (henna) which is a permanent and commercial crop

(Paragraph 4.7.15.3)

There were encroachments on 0.7128 hectare of Government land
valuing & 5.96 crore in Bhilwara Tehsil by various communities but no
effective action was taken for their eviction.

(Paragraph 4.7.16)

In 101 cases of four tehsils, Government land was under encroachment
for industrial use, where penalty of € 0.38 lakh was charged instead of
& 1.03 crore.

(Paragraph 4.7.17)

In 29 cases of three tehsils, Tehsildars charged penalty of only = 240
instead of % 16.54 crore considering the rent of & 33.07 lakh for the
Government land used for mining by the trespassers.

(Paragraph 4.7.18)

In 49 cases of three tehsils, Tehsildars charged penalty of ¥ 970 instead
of & 68.38 lakh trom trespassers for establishing brick kiln industry on
Government land.

(Paragraph 4.7.19)

Khatedari land was used for non-agricultural purposes without conversion of
land resulting in non-recovery of premium for conversion of land amounting
to % 8.11 crore.

(Paragraph 4.10)

Regularisation charges collected on use of agricultural land for non-
agricultural purposes were not remitted in Government Account along with
interest amounting to हैं 1.23 crore.

(Paragraph 4.11)

v.

Stamp Duty and Registration Fee

A Para on “Levy and Collection of Land Tax’ revealed the following:

The AAs neither conducted the survey in their jurisdictional area nor
identified the persons who were liable to pay land tax for the purpose of
preparing a provisional assessment list.

(Paragraph 5.8.5)
The provisional assessment lists were finalised without considering the
objections raised by the assessees and without providing them proper

opportunity of being heard. The final authenticated assessment lists were
not published.

(Paragraph 5.8.6)



Overview

e Escapement from assessment of Land Tax resulted in non-levy of Land
Tax of 7.29 crore.

(Paragraph 5.8.7)

¢ — Issue of incorrect assessment orders resulted in over-assessment of Land
Tax of % 4.87 crore.

(Paragraph 5.8.8.3)
¢ The Assessing Authorities did not impose penalty amounting to
& 16.77 crore on delayed deposit of Land Tax.
(Paragraph 5.8.10)
Non-recovery of Stamp Duty on breach of conditions of Rajasthan Investment
Promotion Scheme, 2003 and 2010 resulted in loss of revenue of € 3.29 crore.
(Paragraph 5.11)
Incorrect valuation and under-valuation of properties on registration of lease
deeds resulted in short levy of Stamp Duty and Registration Fee of हैं 4.86
crore.
(Paragraph 5.12)
Under-valuation of properties due to incorrect application of rates and
incorrect classification of documents resulted in short levy of Stamp Duty and
Registration Fee amounting to हैं 4.91 crore.

(Paragraph 5.14)

Stamp Duty amounting to हैं 6.00 crore was not levied on the documents of
transfer of lease by way of assignment.

(Paragraph 5.15)

Stamp Duty and Registration Fee amounting to € 31.88 crore were not charged
on lease deeds on differential market price of previous land and after its
changed use.

(Paragraph 5.16)

VI. State Excise

A Para on ‘Functioning of Excise Preventive Force’ revealed the following:

e Incorrect entries of raids were made in movement registers and log books.
There was no control mechanism to check the correctness and
genuineness of entries made in such records.

(Paragraph 6.8.4.1)

e Staff of Excise Preventive Force (EPF) Stations treated patrolling and
raids interchangeably and reported all entries made in the movement
register as raids conducted by the EPF Station to higher authorities.
Further, the Department did not periodically examine the list of sensitive
places of production, collection and sale of illicit liquor in the State to
ensure that they indeed continued to be sensitive.

(Paragraph 6.8.4.3)

xi


Audit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

e Patrolling Officers (POs) of selected 48 EPF Stations (out of 52) could
not achieve their targets of detection and registration of the cases i.e. 120
cases per year. The performance of EPF Stations regarding Special Report
cases was dismal.

(Paragraph 6.8.4.4)

e — EPF officials did not make any enquiry to find out the source of supply of
liquor to offenders and the investigation reports ended merely with the
conclusion regarding possession of illicit liquot/hathkad liquor by the
accused. Further, the enquiry officers did not complete investigation even
after lapse of one to five years.

(Paragraph 6.8.4.5)

e There was no system in place in EPF Stations to share or exchange
information regarding habitual offenders and suspected places of illicit
liquor with State Police Department.

(Paragraph 6.8.6)

Wholesale depots of Canteen Store Department (CSD) located at Jaipur and
Bikaner sold 67.98 lakh BL IMFL and 6.88 lakh BL beer to its retail off
licencees (unit run canteens) in the State during the year 2011-12. However,
Special Vend Fee (SVF) of & 6.80 crore on IMFL and हैं 0.34 crore on beer
was neither deposited by the CSD nor demanded by the Department. This
resulted in non-levy of SVF amounting to हैं 7.14 crore.

(Paragraph 6.10.2)
During export of beer under bond by five Breweries outside the State, 1.32
lakh bulk litres (16,233 cartons) beer involving excise duty of 55.27 lakh
were not delivered at the destination. Neither was the duty paid by the
Breweries nor was it demanded by the Department, This resulted in non-levy
of State Excise Duty of f 55.27 lakh.

(Paragraph 6.10.3)

VII. Non-Tax Receipts

Department of Mines and Geology and Department of Petroleum

In six cases, demand of हैं 1.57 crore in respect of 1,25,679 MT mineral
masonry stone unlawfully excavated and despatched from the areas of illegally
subletted leases areas, was neither raised nor recovered.

(Paragraph 7.7.1)

Delay/non-finalisation of excess royalty collection contract/reyalty collection
contract resulted in short recovery of royalty of € 3.47 crore.

(Paragraph 7.7.3)

In 49 cases, cost of F 10.87 crore for mineral marble excavated illegally from
the areas of bapi pattas was not realised. The bapi patta holders were neither
regularised nor evicted by the Department.

(Paragraph 7.7.5)

xii



Overview

Excavation of mineral either without obtaining the short term permit or
beyond the permissible limit resulted in short realisation of revenue of
% 6.70 crore.

(Paragraph 7.7.10)

Recovery of single royalty on mineral illegally removed/despatched instead of
cost of mineral resulted in loss of revenue of f 66.08 crore.

(Paragraph 7.7.11)

The following reasons for variations in 2012-13 as compared to 2011-12 were
reported by the concerned Departments:
Taxes on sales, trade etc.: The increase (17 per cent) was due to intensive

monitoring, check on tax evasion, recovery efforts of the Department, increase in
the rate of tax on some commodities.

Central sales tax: The increase (24 per cent) was due to proper monitoring,
check on tax evasion and recovery efforts of the Department.

State excise: The increase (21 per cent) was mainly due to higher receipts from
sale of malt liquor, foreign liquor and spirits, increase in guarantee amount and
licence fees and effective Excise Preventive Force activities.



Chapter-l: General

Stamp duty and registration fees: The increase (26 per cent) was due to higher
sales of non-judicial stamps, fees for registering documents and court fees and
increase in rates of land fixed by DLC’.

Taxes and duties on electricity: The increase (43 per cent) was due to higher
receipt of taxes on consumption and sale of electricity.

Taxes on motor vehicles: The increase (18 per cent) was mainly due to higher
receipt of taxes, surcharge, green tax etc.

Taxes on goods and passengers: The increase (13 per cent) was mainly due to
higher receipt of entry tax on goods.

1.2 Response of the Government/Department towards Audit
observations

The Accountant General (Economic and Revenue Sector Audit), Rajasthan
conducts periodical inspections of the Government Departments to test check the
transactions and verify the maintenance of important accounts and other records
as prescribed in the rules and procedures. These inspections are followed by
issue of the inspection reports (IRs) incorporating irregularities detected during
the inspections and not settled on the spot, which are issued to the heads of
offices inspected with copies to the next higher authorities for taking prompt
corrective action. The heads of offices/Department are required to promptly
comply with the observations contained in the IRs, rectify the defects and
omissions and report compliance through initial replies to the Accountant
General within one month from the date of issue of the IRs. Serious financial
irregularities are reported to the heads of the Departments and the Government.

observations involving = 7,731.42 crore relating to 2,882 IRs were outstanding
at the end of June 2013. The details of IRs and audit observations outstanding

1.3 Audit planning

The unit offices under various Departments are categorised into high, medium
and low risk units according to their revenue position, past trends of audit
observations and other parameters. The annual audit plan is prepared on the
basis of risk analysis which includes critical issues in Government revenues and
tax administration i.e. budget speech, white paper on State finances, reports of
the Finance Commission (State and Central), recommendations of the taxation
reforms committee, statistical analysis of the revenue earnings during the past

2.1 Tax administration

The Commercial Taxes Department deals mainly with Value Added Tax,
Central Sales Tax, Entry Tax, Luxury Tax, Entertainment Tax and Electricity
Duty which are regulated by the following Acts and Rules made thereunder:

1. Rajasthan Value Added Tax (RVAT) Act, 2003;

Rajasthan Value Added Tax (RVAT) Rules, 2006;

Central Sales Tax (CST) Act, 1956;

Central Sales Tax (CST) Rules, 1957;

Rajasthan Tax on Entry of Goods into Local Areas Act, 1999 ;
Rajasthan Tax on Luxuries (in Hotels and Lodging Houses) Act, 1990;

Rajasthan Entertainments and Advertisements Tax Act, 1957; and

sea NAM PWN

Rajasthan Electricity (Duty) Act, 1962.

The assessment, levy and collection of Value Added Tax (VAT) in Rajasthan
is governed by the RVAT Act, 2003 effective from | April 2006. Prior to this,
Rajasthan Sales Tax (RST) Act, 1994 was in operation which was repealed
with the implementation of RVAT Act. However, the cases pertaining to RST
regime continued to be governed by the RST Act. Besides, CST Act, 1956 and
the rules made thereunder are also in operation for inter-state sales.

The Commissioner Commercial Taxes (CCT) administers the above
mentioned Acts and Rules under the overall control of Secretary, Finance
(Revenue) Department, Government of Rajasthan. The CCT is assisted by 8
Additional Commissioners, 34 Deputy Commissioners (DC) and a Financial
Adviser. For effective implementation of Government policies and taxation
activities, the Commercial Taxes Department (Department) is divided into 14
Zones. The DC is the senior most administrative officer at the zonal level.

filed by him. Any mistakes or errors noticed during scrutiny of the returns by
the Department are to be rectified by the dealer in accordance with provisions
of Section 24 of the RVAT Act, 2003

2.9.1 Introduction

The assessment, levy and collection of Value Added Tax (VAT) and Central
Sales Tax (CST) are governed by the Rajasthan Value Added Tax (RVAT)
Act, 2003, the Central Sales Tax (CST) Act, 1956 and the Rules framed
thereunder. Prior to enactment of RVAT Act, Rajasthan Sales Tax (RST) Act,
1994 and Rules framed thereunder were applicable on sale of goods in any
form within State.

A registered dealer is deemed to be assessed quarterly or annually on the basis
of the returns filed by him in accordance with Section 23 of RVAT Act, 2003

Under the provisions of Rule 27 of the RST Rules and Rule 24 of RVAT
Rules, the dealer is required to pay demand within thirty days of service of
demand notice or as specified in such notice by the Assessing Authority (AA).
If any demand remains outstanding at the end of the year, the same should be
carried forward in next year’s Demand and Collection Register (DCR). In case
of failure on the part of the assessee to pay the amount within the date
mentioned in the demand notice, the Department can recover the unpaid
amount as if these were arrears of land revenue under Section 52 of RST Act
and Section 49 of RVAT Act.

As per Sections 42 to 53 of the RST Act and Sections 38 to 50 of the RVAT
Act, for the purpose of effecting recovery of the demand which is due and
recoverable from any dealer as arrears of land revenue, the Commissioner,
Commercial Taxes (CCT) or his subordinate officers authorised on this behalf,
have all the powers and can perform all the functions under the Rajasthan
Land Revenue (RLR) Act, 1956, which comprise the serving the writ of

16


Chapter-II: Taxes on Sales, Trade, etc.

demand, warrant of attachment and sale of property including the process of
attachment of property.

In cases where the defaulters do not own any property in the State but have
property in some other States, the concerned AA has to forward the demand
cases to the Collectors of the districts of the States in which the defaulters
possess property, as per the provisions of the Revenue Recovery (RR)
Act, 1890.

2.9.2 Organisational setup

The CCT administers the above mentioned Acts and Rules under the overall
control of Secretary, Finance (Revenue) Department, Government of
Rajasthan. The CCT is assisted by 8 Additional Commissioners, 34 Deputy
Commissioners (DC) and a Financial Adviser. For effective implementation of
Government policies and taxation activities, the Commercial Taxes
Department (Department) is divided into 14 Zones. A separate wing for
Inspection, Audit and Recovery (IAR) was formed with the objective of
monitoring the recovery of the old arrears in 2007-08. DCs (Administration)
who is the senior most administrative at the zonal level was responsible for
monitoring the recoveries under the overall control of the Additional
Commissioner, Inspection, Audit and Recovery (LAR).

The assessment and recovery of tax is undertaken by AAs at the level of
Assistant Commissioners (AC)/Commercial Taxes Officers (CTO) and
Assistant Commercial Taxes Officers (ACTO) posted in circles and wards
respectively. For hearing of appeals against the orders passed by AAs there are
11 DCs (Appeals), who are the first appellate authorities.

2.9.3 Audit objectives

The Performance Audit was conducted with a view to ascertain:

e the extent of accumulated arrears and reasons thereof;

e whether the Rules and procedures prescribed in the Acts and Rules for
recovery were scrupulously followed;

e the efficiency and effectiveness of the system to collect the arrears of tax;
and

e whether adequate internal control and monitoring mechanism existed for
prompt realisation of arrears of revenue.

2.9.4 Audit criteria

The audit criteria for Performance Audit were derived from the provisions of
the following Acts, Rules and notifications issued thereunder:

State Laws

e Rajasthan Sales Tax Act, 1954/1994;

e Rajasthan Sales Tax Rules, 1955/1995;
e Rajasthan Value Added Tax Act, 2003;

e Rajasthan Value Added Tax Rules, 2006.

17


Audit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

Central Laws
e Central Sales Tax Act, 1956; and

e Central Sales Tax (Registration and Turnover) Rules, 1957.

2.9.5 Scope and Methodology of Performance Audit

During the period 2007-08 to 2011-12, receipts of the Department increased
from & 7,750.73 crore to 15,766.43 crore (103 per cent). However, during
the same period, the tax arrears also increased from हैं 2,996.96 crore to
= 4,838.04 crore. A Performance Audit on recovery of arrears was, therefore,
conducted between April 2012 and May 2013 with a view to examine the
reasons for accumulation of the arrears of tax and non-recovery thereof by the
Department.

Records of 14 circles’ along with office of the CCT covering the period from
2007-08 to 2011-12 were selected for detailed audit scrutiny. One circle from
each zone was selected to represent the arrear position of the whole
geographical area of the State. In addition to above, Circle Special-II, Jaipur
and office of DC (Appeal) Jaipur-I were selected for pilot study, whose
findings have also been included. There were 51,809 cases of arrears in
selected circles and wards out of which 4,389 cases were selected for audit on
risk basis. The Department produced records pertaining to 3,801 cases which
were examined, 566 files were not produced and 22 files were stated to be
with legal cell.

An Entry Conference was held on 25 October 2012 with CCT and Deputy
Secretary (Tax), Finance Department wherein scope, methodology and
objectives of the Performance Audit were explained. The Factual Statement
was forwarded to the Government and the Commissioner in August 2013. An
Exit Conference was held on 24 October 2013 with Commissioner,
Commercial Taxes and Secretary, Finance (Revenue) Department wherein the
findings of the Performance Audit were discussed. The replies received during
the Exit Conference and at other points of time have been appropriately
considered and incorporated in the relevant paragraphs.

2.9.6 Acknowledgement

The Indian Audit and Accounts Department acknowledges the cooperation
extended by the Commercial Taxes Department, its officers and staff in
providing necessary information and records to audit.

prompt action for recovering the demand so that it did not lapse into arrear.

Chapter-II: Taxes on Sales, Trade, etc.

54 per cent of arrears as on 31 March 2012 were yet to be recovered despite

traceable dealers also increased to % 331.16 crore in 2011-12 as against
% 269.36 crore in 2009-10. The Department was also slow in taking action on
matters where the arrears were to be written off as these remained pending
with the CCT office and the amount of such arrears had increased from
& 43.06 crore as on 31 March 2010 to हैं 68.83 crore as on 31 March 2012.
Further, a major portion of arrears involving € 956.98 crore or 20 per cent of
total amount of arrears pertained to cases pending with DC (Appeal),
Rajasthan Tax Board or Rajasthan High Court/Supreme Court.

During Exit Conference, CCT stated that recovery of arrears had assumed top
priority. He further stated that AAs have been directed to monitor old demands
both age-wise and amount-wise. Regarding court cases, instructions were
being issued to file applications for early hearing.

The audit findings are discussed in succeeding paragraphs.

2.9.9 Deficient mechanism for recovery of demands

2.9.9.1 Non-maintenance of control registers

As per circular dated 3 January 2008 where any demand of tax, penalty and
interest against the dealer is created, it is entered in the DCR and a note to this
effect is made in returns and tax payment register. The AA monitors the
progress of assessment work and collection through DCR. It was noticed that
DCR was being maintained in all selected circles.

However, the Department had not prescribed separate control registers for
monitoring the progress made in recovery of arrears i.e. for issue of demand
notices, details of movable and immovable property of the dealer, stay
register, register of companies under liquidation, Board for Industrial and
Financial Reconstruction (BIFR) and sick companies etc. In absence of these
separate registers, the correctness of the arrears, their timely monitoring and
action taken for recovery of the amount could not be ascertained.

2.9.9.2 Lack of Internal Control Mechanism

A statistical return in the form of Demi-Oftficial (DO) letter was prescribed
vide which each AA had to submit monthly progress of collection and balance
of arrear demands to CCT through DCs (Administration) of the concerned
zone.

It was noticed that the DO letters did not contain the year wise detail of the
arrears that was pending in the circle for more than five years. As such the
year wise action taken for recovery of old arrears could not be monitored. The
DO letters also did not reflect the name of defaulting dealers having highest
arrears of tax against them and their pendency period. Further, monitoring of
recovery of arrears at CTO/DC level was restricted to reporting through
monthly DO letters.

21


Audit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

Scrutiny of records of selected circles disclosed that regular meetings were not
held with AAs by the DCs to monitor the progress of recovery.

The Secretary (Revenue) during Exit Conference in reference to paragraph
2.9.9.1 and 2.9.9.2 suggested that a module ‘Management Information
System’ (MIS) should be introduced in the Department to provide separate
exception reporting of recovery cases. It would help the AAs to take prompt
action. Further action taken has uot been intimated (February 2014).

2.9.9.3 Absence of specific time frame for initiation of recovery
proceedings

It was noticed (August 2013) that no time frame was prescribed in the RVAT
Act, 2003 or RVAT Rules, 2006 or by the CCT (by issue of notification/
circular/office order) to ensure initiation of recovery proceedings by the
specified time ic. for issue of notices for recovery, attachment of bank
accounts, recovery from surety, tracing movable and immovable property,
attachment of such property and auction thereof etc. A few cases of non-
initiation of action under RLR Act are discussed in para no. 2.9.10.

The Department replied (October 2013) that no specific time frame can be
prescribed as there were different types of arrear cases. The recovery in
respect of the cases having outstanding amount of more than one crore is
being watched at Headquarters and information regarding their recovery is
being called monthly and directions are accordingly given for their recovery.

The reply is not acceptable as specific time frame can be prescribed for all
types of cases once these are declared as arrears as this would help in timely
recovery of amounts due to Government.

Chapter-II: Taxes on Sales, Trade, etc.

It was noticed (February and May 2013) that no monitoring was done by the
AAs to ensure that the JCTOs had taken adequate action for attachment of
property of defaulting dealers. In a few cases where the property could be
attached by the Department, the AAs failed to auction them as discussed in
following paragraphs.

2.9.10.1 During scrutiny of recovery files of CTO, Circle Jhunjhunu, it
was noticed (May 2013) that demands of % 26.33 lakh were raised (March
2000) and pending against M/s Manoj Trading Company, Pilani tor the period
1988-89, 1989-90 and 1994-95. The Department identified (February 2003) a
joint property of the dealer in Chirawa Tehsil. The AA issued the warrant of
attachment of property in March 2003. [t was noticed that the AA directed
(April 2003) the CTI to attach the property. However, the CTI on each
occasion asked (May 2003 to October 2009) for further extension for
attachment of property. No further correspondence was found in the
recovery file.

The Government replied (February 2014) that demands of = 26.27 lakh for the
year 1988-89 and 1989-90 were set aside by DC (Appeal) Bikaner in
November 2005 and demand of % 0.06 lakh for the year 1994-95 was
recoverable. The reply is not acceptable as the Department had shown the
amount as recoverable in their records and warrant of attachment of property
was issued in August 2009 for recovering the demands of € 26.33 lakh.

2.9.10.2 During scrutiny of recovery files of CTO, Circle Jhunjhunu, it
was noticed (May 2013) that the AA had attached immovable property
(factory and machinery) of M/s Surajgarh Chemicals & Fertilisers Pvt. Ltd,
Surajgarh in May 2002 against a demand of % 14.28 lakh raised (August 2002)
for the period 1992 to 1999. The AA issued numbers of notices for auction of
factory and machinery between May 2002 and February 2013 but failed to
attract any bidders.

The Government replied (February 2014) that हैं 8.66 lakh had been recovered
in October 2013 and the dealer has applied to CCT for waiver of remaining
demand.

2.9.10.3 During scrutiny of recovery files of AC, Circle-B Bharatpur, it
was noticed (February 2013) that the AA had attached (April 1996)
immoveable property of M/s Shankarlal Omprakash, Bharatpur against a
demand of ¥ 23.82 lakh for the period 1994-95. Though the AA held repeated
auctions for the attached property between August 1996 and September 2011,
no bidder participated in the auction proceedings. No action was found to have
been taken after September 2011.

The Government accepted (February 2014) the facts and stated that auction
could not take place as no bidders participated in the auction process.

2.9.10.4 During scrutiny of recovery files of CTO, Circle Jhunjhunu, it
was noticed (May 2013) that the AA attached immovable property of
M/s Jeevanram Birbalram, against a demand of € 4.58 lakh raised in March
1994 for the period 1988 to 1990. The AA held repeated auctions of the
property between February 1996 and July 2010 but the property could not be
sold. No further correspondence was found in the recovery file.

23


Audit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

The Government stated (February 2014) that despite adequate publicity, the
property could not be auctioned. The reply also stated that next auction date
for sale of property has been fixed for 13 February 2014.

The Department even after lapse of considerable time failed to recover arrears
from dealers whose properties were identified and attached.

Despite repeated failure of the Department in auctioning of the attached
property, no system was evolved to identify the reasons or deficiencies behind
such failure. An appropriate system of auction has to be developed to ensure
revenue realisation.

The CCT during Exit Conference stated that cases pending under LR Act were
being regularly pursued and instructions would again be issued to speed up
progress in finalisation of the above cases.

2.9.11 _Non-recovery of arrears from untraceable dealers

There is no provision in the Act or Rules for obtaining information regarding
movable and immovable property of the dealers and their sureties at the time
of registration. As such the Department had not maintained any records
regarding the movable and immovable property of the dealers and their
sureties to recover the arrears under RLR Act. As a result, the Department
could not trace the dealers or property of the dealers who defaulted trom the
payment of tax due. [t was noticed that the AAs did not take prompt action on
default in six cases but kept on issuing assessment orders on ex-parte basis for
two to three years and thereafter did not make any efforts to trace the dealers
through the JCTOs. However, by then, the dealers had closed their businesses.

As per the information furnished by the Department, € 331.16 crore were
outstanding against the dealers whose whereabouts were not available with the
Department. A few of these cases involving arrears liability of € 5.43 crore are
discussed in the following paragraphs.

2.9.11.1 During test check of recovery files of AC, Circle B, Jaipur, it
was noticed (December 2012 and August 2013) that a dealer M/s Subhiksha
Trading Services Ltd, Jaipur was registered in May 2007 and assessed (March
2010 and January 2011) with a demand of € 1.03 crore for the period 2007-08
and 2008-09. The dealer did not deposit the demand and the demand notices
were returned unserved. The JCTO reported (June 2011) that the firm was not
existing at the given address and that no information was available regarding
Manager’s residence.

The AA could not trace any movable and immovable property of the dealer.
The dealer was thereafier categorised as untraceable. No further efforts under
RLR Act, were found to have been taken to trace whereabouts of the dealer.
This resulted in loss of recoverable arrear of € 1.03 crore including penalty of
₹ 0.67 lakh.

Scrutiny of the file further disclosed that the dealer had become a defaulter in
the year of registration (2007) itself. Had the AA taken prompt action in the
first year of default, the accumulation of arrears could have been avoided.

24


Chapter-II: Taxes on Sales, Trade, etc.

The Department replied (December 2013) that demand of € 47.34 lakh was
reduced after verification of Input Tax Credit (ITC). The Government replied
(February 2014) that notices had been issued to the directors.

2.9,01.2 During test check of recovery files of AC, Circle B, Kota, it
was noticed (May 2012 and August 2013) that a dealer M/s Arvind Traders,
Kota had not deposited the demand of € 3.37 crore assessed for the period
2001-02 and 2002-03. The demand notices were returned unserved
(July 2004). The AA directed (March 2008) the JCTO to enquire about the
whereabouts of the dealer and his property. The JCTO reported
(February 2009) that the dealer had closed his business four years back and
was untraceable. No further action was taken to trace the sureties or the
properties of the dealer.

The Government replied (February 2014) that amount of € 0.55 lakh had been
recovered [rom sureties and National Savings Certificate.

2.9.1.3 During test check of recovery files of AC, Circle-A, Jaipur, it
was noticed (April 2012 and August 2013) that a dealer M/s S Roberts
Pharmaceuticals, Jaipur was in arrears of & 22.86 lakh for the period 2002 to
2005 and assessed between March 2005 and March 2007. The AA issued
notices for recovery in July and August 2012. However, these were returned
unserved. The AA could not trace either the dealer or his property. The AA
also failed to trace the whereabouts of the sureties. Thereafter, the dealer was
declared as untraceable.

The Government replied (February 2014) that notices for recovery were being
issued to the sureties.

2.9.11.4 During test check of recovery files of AC, Circle-A, Jaipur.
was noticed (August 2013) that a dealer M/s Almizan Leather Private Limited,
Jaipur was in arrears of € 11.97 lakh for the period 1985 to 1988. Scrutiny of
file disclosed that the AA wrote (April 1992, July 1994 and May 2013) to the
Collector, Jaipur for pursuing recovery under RR Act, 1890 through Collector,
Chennai as property of the dealer was situated in Chennai. The AA made
correspondence only thrice in twenty years with the Collector, Jaipur.
However, no recovery could be made. The dealer was thereafter declared
untraceable.

The Government replied (February 2014) that further progress would be
intimated shortly.

2.9.11.5 During test check of recovery files of AC, Circle-Special,
Sriganganagar, it was noticed (August 2013) that a dealer M/s Roshanlal
Rajeshkumar, Sadulshahar was in arrears of €2.97 lakh for the period 2002-03

and 2003-04. The Commercial Taxes Inspector (CTD reported (January 2004
and March 2006) that the dealer had closed his business in May 2003 and that
all partners of the firm had become insolvent and untraceable. The AA issued
(December 2005) notices to the sureties and recovered हैं 0.10 lakh from one
surety. However, no recovery could be made from the dealer as no property of
the dealer could be identified. Thereafter, the AA asked (August 2011 and
February 2013) the CTO, Hanumangarh for encashment of National Savings
Certificate and surety bonds as the registration certificate was issued by that

25


Audit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

office. However, no response was received from CTO, Hanumangarh till
March 2013.

The Government replied (February 2014) that efforts were being made
through LR authorities to trace out the property of the dealer.

2.9.11.6 During test check of assessment files of CTO, Circle-B,
Udaipur, it was noticed (February 2013) that M/s C K Daftari & Associates,
Udaipur, was in arrears of € 65.35 lakh as per assessment orders (February
2010) for the period 2001 to 2003. Scrutiny disclosed that only two notices
were issued (March 2010) to the dealer for depositing the demands after the
assessment. The dealer had not deposited the due tax as a result of which
demand of हैं 65.35 lakh along with interest remained unrecovered. The
Department replied (October 2013) that the dealer had closed his business and
was categorised as untraceable.

As stated earlier, the AAs were not prompt in taking action for recovery of
demand. The Department was not having a mechanism to regularly check the
status of dealers and their sureties after the registration certificate was issued.
The property details of the dealers were also not available with the
Department.

The Department replied (October 2013) that efforts to trace out the
whereabouts of the dealers were being made. The Government replied
(February 2014) that notices were again issued to the partners and sureties.

However, in absence of any details of the defaulting/absconding dealers the
chances of recovery of amounts due to Government appear remote.

2.9.12.4 During test check of recovery files of AC, Circle Anti-Evasion-I,

to 2006-07, which included हैं 7.31 crore of taxes and हैं 4.28 crore of interest

A few notices were issued in the years 2007 and 2008 but the dealer did not

except in August 2012 but the dealer refused to acknowledge the same. The
AA also made efforts through the CTO, Circle A, Ajmer, who reported
(August 2012) that the dealer had closed his business and was not
acknowledging the notices. It was also found from the departmental order
sheet (September 2012) that the dealer once expressed his interest to take up
the matter in Tax Settlement Board but no action was found on record to have
been taken by the Department.

The Department had unnecessarily taken a very long time to finalise the
assessments. Had assessments been made on timely basis, when the business
of dealer was running, the demands could have been recovered.

The Government reiterated (February 2014) its earlier reply given in October
2013 that warrant of attachment of property of dealer had been sent (October
2012) to DC (Administration), Ajmer for recovery.

2.9.13 Non-recovery of arrears from tax collection contractors

As per Section 79 of RST Act, 1994 and Section 77 of RVAT Act,
2003, the Commissioner may, permit a contractor to collect tax at the
rates as may be notified from time to time. Rule 44 (i) of RVAT Rules
provides that the contractor shall deposit the entire tax collected or the
1/52" part of the annual tax revenue, whichever is higher, on every
Monday. Where the contractor fails to deposit such amount in the
above manner, AA shall proceed to recover such amount as if such
amount was a demand of tax under the Act. Further, a requirement
which existed during erstwhile RST regime for furnishing a status
certificate regarding holding property equivalent to 25 per cent of the
contract value certified by competent LR officer has been dispensed
with. Further, as per Rule 44 (j) if the contractor violates any condition,
the contract shall be liable to be cancelled by the CCT after affording
opportunity of being heard to the contractor.

During scrutiny of records of selected circles, it was noticed that contractors
engaged in collection of tax at check posts did not deposit the
due amount on prescribed dates and the Department did not take prompt action
to recover the tax. It was further noticed that contracts of these contractors
were also not cancelled on time. This resulted in accumulation of arrears
amounting to हैं 5.11 crore in eight cases in four Circles’, as discussed below:

2.9.13.1 On scrutiny of records of CTO, Circle-B Udaipur, it was
noticed (March 2013) that M/s Jaswant Singh and Party, Jodhpur was awarded
(July 2000) a contract for collection of tax on bazri in revenue Tehsils Mavali,
Vallabhnagar, Salumber and Girwa, Udaipur District for the peried from
16 July 2000 to 15 July 2002 for € 1.07 crore for the first year and हैं 1.18 crore
for the second year. The contractor deposited = 1.03 crore with delays at
various stages during the contract period. The AA passed (March 2002 and
September 2002) assessment orders for the contract period and raised a
demand of % 1.41 crore (including interest of € 0.19 crore). Thereafter, हैं 0.38
crore was recovered/adjusted and & 1.03 crore was in arrears as on May 2005.
The contractor filed an appeal in the Hon’ble Rajasthan High Court which
directed (August 2004) the CCT to decide the controversy expeditiously.
It was noticed that the Department fixed 12 hearing dates between May 2005

* Circles: B-Bhiwadi, B-Kota, Makrana and B-Udaipur,

The Government replied (February 2014) that further action was being taken.

amount.

been realised.

2.9.17 Arrears due to non-verification of deposits

As per Rule 45 of RVAT Rules, 2006 where a dealer or a person
furnishes a challan in Form VAT-37 or a receipt in Form VAT-38 or
Form VAT-39 in order to claim an adjustment of the amount covered by
such challan/receipt and if such adjustment is not made for want of
proper verification of such deposits from the records of check post or
Revenue Collection Register (RCR) and in case of tax deducted at
source (TDS), after verification of VAT-41(Certificate of deduction at
source by awarder), the AA himself shall get the verification done and
shall not enforce the demand to that extent. As per departmental circular
dated 26 April 2004 all AAs shall maintain RCR in the prescribed
format with effect from 1 April 2004 and adjustment of deposits should

be allowed only after verification with RCR.

During scrutiny of records of selected circles it was noticed that demands of
& 1.34 crore were outstanding due to non-vyerification of amount shown as
deposited at check posts, by challans or adjustments of TDS as discussed
below:

2.9.17.1 185 deposited at check post

During test check of records, it was noticed (December 2012 to April 2013)
that in 18 cases of three Circles” the dealers claimed credit of
₹ 64.88 lakh in their returns stating that advance tax had been deposited by
them at the departmental check posts for the period from 2003-04 to 2009-10
(assessed between December 2006 and December 2011). As per Rule 45 of
RVAT Rules, the AAs were required to get the verification done from the
check posts records. However, they did not allow such credit for want of
verification and raised demand to that extent. It was noticed that the
verification reports were not obtained even after lapse of period ranging
between three and nine years.

The Government replied (October 2013) that demand of f 14.28 lakh had been
verified and adjusted in three cases and directions were being issued to AAs
for verification of tax deposited at check posts.

2.9.17.2 185 deposited by challans or by TDS

During test check of records of selected circles, it was noticed (May 2013) that
adjustment of deposits by challans/TDS of % 69.32 lakh was not allowed by
the AAs for want of verification with RCR or TDS from concerned circles in
11 cases at Circle B-Bhiwadi and Jhunjhunu for the period 2006-07 to
2009-10 (assessed between February 2009 and February 2012). The
verifications should have been done by the AAs immediately before the
assessment in these 11 cases but the delay in verification ranged between three
and six years.

5 Cireles: B-Bharatpur, B-laipur and Makrana,

38


Chapter-II: Taxes on Sales, Trade, etc.

The Government replied (October 2013) that directions were being issued to
AAs for verification of tax deducted at source. The Government further
replied (February 2014) that an amount of € 26.71 lakh in six cases had been
verified and adjusted.

2.9.18 Non-recovery of arrears under CST Act

A dealer who claims partial or full exemptions from payment of tax on
sale/transfer of goods shall furnish declaration form. As per Rule 12 of
the CST (Registration & Turnover) Rules, 1957, the declaration in Form
“C’, Form ‘F’ and the Certificate ‘E-I or E-II’, as the case may be, shall
be furnished within three months after the end of the period to which the
declaration or the certificate relates. Further, the declaration
form ‘H’ shall be furnished upto the time of assessment by the first
Assessing Authority.

During scrutiny of records of selected circles, it was noticed (April 2012 to
May 2013) that in 178 cases of 13 Circles’, demands of € 10.51 crore and
interest of € 2.31 crore were outstanding for recovery due to non-submission
of CST declaration forms pertaining to the period 1992-93 to 2009-10.

The declaration forms were required to be submitted by the dealers within
three months after the end of the period to which the declaration or the
certificate relates. However, the same were not submitted and accordingly
demands were raised at the prescribed rates instead of concessional rate by the
AAs. It was also noticed that the dealers had not asked for any extension of
time for submission of forms.

It was further noticed that seven dealers of two circles against whom demand
of % 59.13 lakh was outstanding for want of CST forms had closed their
businesses between 2004-05 and 2010-11. The AAs had, however, not made
any efforts to recover the demand against these dealers except for issuing
routine demand notices.

The Government replied (October 2013) that demands of % 3.74 crore were
adjusted in 31 cases.

The CCT during Exit Conference stated that it was within the competency of
the AAs as per the Act to allow extension in submission of CST forms and
that the Department had started recoveries in cases where no time extension
was allowed.

The reply of the Department is not correct as audit has pointed out only those
cases which were finalised by the AAs and rates as prescribed by the State
Government were applied i.e. treating the sales without supporting declaration
form. But in none of these cases action for recovery was taken.

Rajsamand, Sitohi, Special Si

2.9.19 Non-recovery of arrears under RST/RVAT Act

A dealer who claims partial or full exemptions from payment on

on sale/transfer of goods shall furnish declaration form. As per Rule
23 of RST Rules, 1995 the dealer shall furnish declaration forms
with the return/s or at the time of assessment or within six months
from the date of assessment as AA may allow. Further, the AA may
allow, for reasons to be recorded in writing, a further period of
six month for furnishing of declaration form such as ‘ST-17’ or
“ST-17B’, and other certificates as prescribed, along with his returns.

Further, as per Rule 21 of RVAT Rules, 2006, the dealer shall be
required to furnish declaration forms such as VAT-15 and other
certificates as prescribed, along with the returns or prior to the due
date of annual returns or audit reports, as the case may be.

The State Government from time to time amended Rule 21 of RVAT
Rules at its own volition and extended the period for submission

of the pending RST/RVAT declaration forms/certificates up to
June 2013. wy,

During scrutiny of records of selected circles, it was noticed (April 2012 to
May 2013) that in 64 cases of nine Circles’, demand of % 2.09 crore and
interest of F 1.13 crore were outstanding recovery for the period 1993-94 to
2009-10 due to non-submission of declaration forms/certificates under
RST/RVAT up to June 2013, The Department by giving continuous extension
of time for submission of RST/RVAT forms to the defaulting dealers has
prompted non-adherence to its own framed Rules/Act.

It was further noticed that seven dealers of three selected circles against whom
demands of हैं 67.86 lakh were pending for non submission of RST/RVAT
declaration forms had closed down their businesses between 2000-01 and
2009-10. The AAs had, however, not made any efforts to recover the demand
against these dealers other than issuing routine demand notices.

The Government replied (October 2013) that demand of हैं 31.00 lakh were
adjusted in six cases.

The Department should review the position and fix a final deadline, as
allowing extension in submission of declaration forms, which are pending
since 1993-94, is not in the interest of revenue.

Chapter-Il: Taxes on Sales, Trade, etc.

2.9.20 Non-lodging of interest claims before Official Liquidator
(OL)

As per Section 55 of RVAT Act, 2003, where any person or a dealer
commits a default in making the payment of any amount of tax, fee,
penalty, interest or any other amount payable by him, he shall be liable to
pay interest on such amount at such rate as notified by the State
Government from time to time.

The OLs are officers appointed by the Central Government under Section 448
of the Companies Act and are attached to various High Courts. The primary
function of the OL is to administer the assets of companies under liquidation,
sale of the assets and realisation of all debts of companies under liquidation
for the purpose of distributing the same among the various creditors and other
share holders of the companies and to finally dissolve the company after such
affairs are completed.

During test check of records of CTO, Special-I Circle, Jaipur, it was noticed
(February 2012) that two companies had wound up in August 2003 (M/s Kesti
Vanaspati Products Limited, Jaipur) and December 2006 (M/s Pooja Granite
and Marbles Limited, Jaipur). However, further scrutiny of files disclosed that
the AA had not included the interest amount of € 4.75 crore in respect of
M's Kesri Vanaspati Products Limited, Jaipur and = 1.11 crore in respect of
M’s Pooja Granite and Marbles Limited, Jaipur when claims for recovery of
arrears were lodged before the OL on 27 October 2006 and 6 January 2007
respectively. This resulted in loss of interest of f 5.86 crore.

The Government replied (February 2014) that a revised claim including
interest had been sent to the OL in case of M/s Kesri Vanaspati Products
Limited, Jaipur and in case of M/s Pooja Granite and Marbles Limited, Jaipur
submission of interest claim was under process.

2.9.21 Non-realisation of arrears of tax due to inadequate
follow-up with Debt Recovery Tribunal (DRT)

DRT has been constituted under Section 3 of the Recovery of Debts due to
Banks and Financial Institutions Act, 1993. If the dealer’s property is under
the possession of the DRT, the Department has to lodge its claim before
the DRT.

During test check of recovery files of ACTO, Ward-III, Circle E, Jaipur it was
noticed (August 2013) that a demand of 1.99 crore was outstanding against
M/s Pratap Rajasthan Special Steel Ltd, Jaipur for the period 1994-95 to
2000-01. The case was categorised as ‘under stay’ by the AA for the period
from March 2000 to April 2003. However, as per the JCTO’s report
(June 2012) the DRT had sold out the movable and immovable property of the
company in 2005 to a number of units and the sale proceeds had been
distributed among financial institutions.

41



Audit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

The Government replied (October 2013) that concerned AA had been directed
to expedite the matter. The chances of recovery from the company are remote
as no other assets of the dealer are now available.

2.9.22 Non-realisation of arrears due to non-vacation of stay in
cases pending with Rajasthan High Court and Rajasthan
Taxation Tribunal

A legal cell was created in the office of the CCT to monitor all cases pending
with the Rajasthan High Court and other Courts. Scrutiny of recovery files of
two circles disclosed that in two cases, the AA did not take adequate measures
promptly to get the embargo lifted on recoveries. As a result, the arrears
amounting to हैं 3.39 crore and interest thereon remained blocked as detailed
below:

2.9.22.1 During scrutiny of records of CTO, Special Circle, Ajmer it
was noticed (December 2011) that a case of M/s Koyana Rocla Pipes Ltd,
Kekri was pending with the Rajasthan Taxation Tribunal (Tribunal). It was
found that demands of 3.22 crore relating to the years 1989-90 to 1997-1998
were raised between 1993-94 and 2000-01, but could not be recovered due to
restriction on coercive steps (stay) by orders of the Tribunal. Though, more
than 15 years have elapsed since the orders of the Tribunal no effective steps
were taken by the Department for vacation of stay and recovery of arrear.
Further, the factual report on the case was provided to the Government
Advocate after 12 years in January 2010.

In the year 1999, the Tribunal was abolished and the case was transferred to
Rajasthan High Court. However, there was no change in the status and the stay
remained in force (February 2014).

Thus, lack of prompt action by the Department resulted in non- recovery of
demand of ¥ 3.22 crore and interest thereon.

The Government accepted (February 2014) that the case was still under stay
and stated that the next date of hearing was yet to be intimated.

2.9,22.2 During scrutiny of records of AC, Circle Rajsamand, it was
noticed (March 2013) that in the case of M/s Saraswati Marble and Granite
Industries, a demand of % 17.02 lakh for the year 1996-97 was raised in
February 2004. The Hon’ble Rajasthan High Court passed (June 2004) a stay
order that the Department shall not adopt any coercive measure for recovery.
As per the recovery file, the last hearing of the case was fixed on 2 March
2005 and thereafter, no further details were found. Inaction by the AA to
pursue the case resulted in non-recovery of demand of & 17.02 lakh and
interest thereon.

The Government replied (February 2014) that efforts were being made to get
the stay vacated.

42


Chapter-II: Taxes on Sales, Trade, etc.

2.9.23 Internal Control

Internal controls are intended to provide reasonable assurance of proper
enforcement of laws, rules, executive instructions etc. Such control is
exercised through internal audit and periodical effective inspection by the
supervisory officers.

Deficiencies in the system of periodical review of the arrear cases and
monitoring by the senior officers coupled with lack of coordination of AA
with the Land Revenue Officers, BIFR, OL and other State officers for inter-
State recoveries led to non-recovery of arrears as have been pointed out in
some of the preceding paragraphs. Further, deficiencies noticed in the internal
control mechanism are discussed below:

2.9.23.1 Internal Audit

As per Para 19 (Chapter-3) of Internal Audit Manual, Internal Audit was
required to audit all the recovery cases involving demand of more than
रू one crore to reveal the deficiencies in the efforts to recover the dues and to
fix responsibilities on officers concerned in the defaulting cases.

Scrutiny of internal audit reports of the selected circles revealed (April 2012 to
May 2013) that no observation was made regarding non-recovery of arrears of
revenue in the selected circles. Resultantly, the Department could not identify
the deficiencies in the system and put in place the required preventive
measures.

The Department replied (October 2013) that separate instructions had been
issued to [AW for recovery of arrears and to fix responsibilities in this regard.

2.9,.23.2 Non-revision of additional security deposit of dealers

Section 15 of RVAT Act provides that the AA shall require the dealer to
furnish the initial security or such additional security as may be considered
necessary for timely payment of the amount of tax or other sum payable by
him. Further, the amount of security, in case of a dealer who is already
registered may be increased by the AA, for reasons to be recorded in writing,
to 25 per cent of the annual tax liability of the immediate preceding year and
for the dealers registered in the current year such increase in the security
amount may be 25 per cent of the highest tax liability of the preceding months
or the quarters, as the case may be.

However, it was noticed (July 2013) that in none of the selected circles, the
AA asked the dealers to deposit the security deposit to the extent of
25 per cent of the annual tax liability, of the immediate preceding year in case
of a dealer who was already registered and to the extent of 25 per cent of the
highest tax liability of the preceding months or the quarters, in case of a dealer
who registered in the current year. In the absence of proper security, the AAs
were not in a position to realise the arrears if any of the dealers defaulted.

It was further noticed that the CCT had issued (July 2007) directions that the
security amount of dealers who had defaulted in making timely payment of tax
should be increased but the AAs did not comply with the direction and the
senior officers did not monitor its implementation.

43


Audit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

The Department replied (October 2013) that security deposit was revised as
and when required. The reply is not acceptable as no such case was noticed
during the conduct of this performance audit.

2.9.24 | Non-production of records

Copies of returns filed by the dealers, orders passed by AAs, notices served to
the dealers and other correspondences are filed in assessment files for each
year in respect of each dealer. These files are the basis for initiation of
recovery proceeding provided under the Acts in cases where there are arrears
of revenue.

During review of records of 12 Circles*, recovery records of 566 cases
pertaining to the period 1969 to 2010 were not produced to Audit.

Non-availability of recovery records will adversely affect the pursuance of
cases for recovery of arrears and will adversely impact recovery of
& 25.96 crore.

The CCT agreed during Exit Conference that record keeping in the
Department was poor and stated that efforts were now being made to keep the
records in proper shape.

2.9.25 Conclusion

The Performance Audit on ‘Recovery of arrears in Commercial Taxes
Department’ disclosed a number of deficiencies, which were having adverse
impact on recovery of arte: The Department did not prescribe control
registers to monitor progress of recovery. In addition, no time frame was
prescribed for initiation of recovery proceedings. Status certificate and sureties
were not ascertained at the time of issue of registration certificate. There were
abnormal delays in attachment of property and auction thereof with the result
that dealers were not traceable after winding up their business, with huge tax
demands pending against them. The officials of the Department were slack in
pursuing recovery of old dues, resulting in pendency of old demands for years
together, though such dealers were paying the current demands regularly.
Action under Revenue Recovery Act, 1890 and Land Revenue Act, 1956 were
unimpressive and amount of arrears was piling up due to non-verification of
ITC/tax deposited at check post or by challans and non-submission of
declaration forms. The Department also did not pursue the cases with High
Court Official Liquidator/Debt Recovery Tribunal and other authorities
properly with the result that revenue got locked up in litigation.
Non-revision of security deposits and deficiencies in internal control and.
internal audit system also affected the performance of the Department as
regards recovery of arrears.

* Circles: B-Bharatpur, B-Bhiwadi, A-Jaipur, B-Jaipur, E-Jaipur, Special-II Jodhpur, B-Kota, Makrana, Rajsamand
Sirohi, B-Udaipur and Anti-Evasion -II Rajasthan Jaipur

44


Chapter-II: Taxes on Sales, Trade, etc.

2.9.26 _ Recommendations

The Government may consider:

prescribing targets for recovery of arrears for the Assessing
Authorities;

Prescribing control registers to allow for proper follow up and
monitoring of recovery of arrears of revenue by CCT on a monthly
basis;

identifying causes for not being able to auction properties of
defaulting dealers;

ensuring that there is a close monitoring over submission of returns by
the dealers, timely issue of demand notices by responsible officers and
on the status of business of the dealers as reported by the JCTOs;

evolving a practical and time bound monitoring system by the
Department and the Collector of the district for issuing RRCs in time
and regularly coordinating with the Collectors in other States;

reviewing its practices of granting extensions time and again for
submission of the declaration forms in the interest of prompt
realisation of its dues from the dealers; and

fixing targets for examination of arrear cases by the [AW to collect the
huge revenues of f 4,838.04 crore outstanding and due to it.

During test check of the assessment records of VAT in the Commercial Taxes
Department several cases of non-observance of provisions of Acts/Rules,
non-levy/short levy of tax/interest, incorrect computation of tax were
observed. Some of these omissions were pointed out in earlier years also.
However, not only do the irregularities persist, these also remain undetected
until an audit is conducted. There is need for the Government to improve the
internal control system including strengthening of internal audit so that
recurrence af such cases can be avoided.

2.15 Irregular/excess grant of subsidy

The State Government vide notification dated
28 July 2003, introduced the Rajasthan

Investment Promotion Scheme, 2003 (RIPS)
to attract investors to Rajasthan. As per para
7(i)(b) of the RIPS, in case of investment
made in expansion, the amount of subsidy
shall be subject to a maximum of 50 per cent
(75 per cent where the investments exceed
(® 200 crore) of the additional amount of
RST/VAT and CST payable or deposited by
the unit over and above the highest tax
payable or deposited whichever is higher, in
any of the three preceding years i.e., base year.

As per Section 17(1) of RVAT Act, 2003, tax
payable by a dealer shall be calculated by
adding the amount of output tax, reverse tax
and purchase tax less the amount of ITC.

Further, as per Section 18(1) of RVAT Act,
2003 ITC shall be allowed to registered
dealers, in respect of purchases of any taxable
goods made within the State from a registered
dealer for the purposes as mentioned in the
sub section and includes the purpose of sale
in the course of export outside the territory
of India.

It was also clarified vide notification dated 10
October 2008 for RIPS that the tax payable by
the units availing benefit of RIPS is the
amount of tax leviable less the amount of ITC.
Besides, breach of any of the conditions shall
make the subsidy amount liable to be
recovered as tax along with interest at the rate
of 18 per cent per annum from the date from

which subsidy was provided as per paragraph
10 of the RIPS. yy,

2.16.2 During test check
of the assessment records
of the CTO, Special Circle,

(January 2013) that a dealer
(M/s Peacock Industries,

percentage of deferment of tax in the

है of the tax payable during

the year 2009-10. The

dealer, however, claimed 40 per cent deferment of tax during the year treating
it as an extended period of the scheme. The AA, while finalising
(January 2012) the assessment for the year 2009-10, allowed 40 per cent
deferment of tax of हैं 38.05 lakh, as claimed by the dealer against the
allowable deferment of tax amounting to हैं 9.51 lakh only. This resulted in
excess deferment of tax of € 28.54 lakh on which interest of हैं 8.56 lakh
(calculated upto March 2012) is leviable.

The omission was pointed out to the Department (February 2013) and reported
to Government (April 2013). The Government intimated (August 2013) that as
per notification dated 31 March 2006 benefit of consolidated deferment was
allowable for five years only with effect from 1 April 2006 and there was no
provision in the notification for restricting yearly tax deferment benetit by
10 per cent. It was further stated that the dealer had been allowed benefit at the
rate of 40 per cent as per paragraph 6 of notification dated 31 March 2006.
The reply as furnished by the Department is not in line with the provisions of
the notification dated 31 March 2006 which stipulates that ‘remaining eligible
period under the old scheme shall be extended equal to the remaining
eligibility period subject to a maximum of five years’. Accordingly the
extended period will start after the completion of already sanctioned period of
the scheme and provisions of paragraph 6 are applicable for the extended
period only. In this case, the year 2009-10 was within the original sanctioned
(remaining) period. Therefore, the dealer was entitled for deferment at the rate
of 10 per cent only as mentioned in Annexure-B of the scheme as per
notification.

55


| CHAPTER-III: TAXES ON MOTOR VEHICLES

3.1 Tax administration

The Transport Department is responsible for registration of vehicles, grant of
permits for vehicles and exercise of control over vehicles plying in the State.
The Department also issues licences to drivers, conductors and traders and
fitness certificate of vehicles. Levy and collection of taxes, fees and penalties
under the provisions of the Motor Vehicles Act, 1988, the Central Motor
Vehicles Rules, 1989, the Rajasthan Motor Vehicles Taxation Act
(RMVT Act), 1951, the Rajasthan Motor Vehicles Taxation Rules
(RMVT Rules), 1951 and the Rajasthan Motor Vehicles Rules, 1990 are other
responsibilities of the Department. Works relating to registration and fitness of
vehicles, grant of licences, collection of taxes, fees, penalties etc. have been
computerised in the Department.

The Transport Department is headed by the Transport Commissioner who is
assisted by 5 Additional Transport Commissioners and 13 Deputy Transport
Commissioners. The entire State is divided into 11 regions, headed by
Regional Transport Officers (RTO) cum ex-officio Member, Regional
Transport Authority. Besides, there are 37 vehicles registration cum taxation
offices headed by District Transport Officers (DTO).

60


Chapter-IlI: Taxes on Motor Vehicles

3.8 Audit observations

During test check of records of Transport Department, we noticed several
cases of non-levy of tax, fee and penalty. Some of these omissions were also
pointed out in earlier years. However, it is seen that similar irregularities
persist year afier year and remain undetected till the next audit is conducted.
These cases are illustrative and are based on a test check carried out by Audit.
It was observed that no computerised system existed in the Department to
monitor proper maintenance af tax ledgers of registered vehicles to ensure
recovery of tax, fee and other charges. Besides, no return was prescribed to
show the number of vehicles from which tax was due but not received. There is
need to improve the internal control system including strengthening of internal
audit and putting in place a monitoring mechanism hy way of periodical
returns to ensure collection of tax, fee etc.

3.9 Non-compliance of provisions of the Acts/Rules

The RMVT Act, RMVT Rules and notifications provide, inter alia, for:
(9

levy and collection af tax on all mator vehicles used or kept for use in the
State at the rates prescribed by the State Government from time to time;

(ii) levy of lump sum tax on transport vehicles at the rates prescribed hy the
State Government from time to time; and
(iii) levy of special road tax and surcharge on tax at the rate prescribed.

During test check of the records, it was noticed that Departmental authorities
did not observe some of the above provisions in cases mentioned in paragraphs

3.9.1 to 3.9.3.

| CHAPTER-

LAND REVENUE

4.1 185 administration

Assessment and collection of land revenue are governed under the Rajasthan
Land Revenue Act, 1956 and rules made thereunder. Land revenue mainly
comprises rent on land, lease rent, premium, conversion charges and receipts
from sales of Government land.

The Revenue Department functions as the Administrative Department of the
Government and it administers all matters relating to assessment and
collection of land revenue. The overall control of revenue related judicial
matters along with supervision and monitoring over revenue officers vests
with the Board of Revenue (BOR). The BOR is assisted by 33 Collectors at
the district level, 242 SDOs at the sub-division level and 244 Tehsildars at the
Tehsil level. The BOR is also the State level implementing authority for
computerisation of land records in Rajasthan.

4.2 Trend of revenue

the period 2008-09 to 2012-13 were as under:

respectively.

receipts of the State during the year 2012-13, as against 0.82 per cent during
2011-12. Thus, there has been an erratic trend in collection of revenue under
this head though the total tax receipts of the State continuously show an
increasing trend.

4.7.1 Introduction

The Government land is managed under the provisions of the Rajasthan Land.
Revenue (RLR) Act, 1956 and rules made thereunder. As per Section 88 of the
RLR Act all roads etc. and lands which are not the property of other belongs to
the State and it is lawful for the Collector to dispose of them as per prescribed
procedures/rules. The Collector is the principal land record officer of a district.
He is further assisted by the 51005 at the sub-division level and by Tehsildar/
Nath Tehsildar at the Tehsil level.

Land, being a scarce and limited resource needs to be used efticiently by the
State Government. Unauthorised occupation of Government land and its
eviction is dealt with under Section 91 of the RLR Act, 1956, Section 22 of
Rajasthan Colonisation (RC) Act, 1954 and Rajasthan Land Revenue (Eviction
of Trespasser) Rules, 1975. Cases of encroachment against trespassers are, at
first instance, either filed under Section 91 of RLR Act or under Section 22 of
RC Act, 1954 on the report of Pafwari and thereafter disposed of in the court
of Tehsildar/Naib Tehsildar afier affording an opportunity of being heard to
trespassers.

The encroachments on Government land can broadly be bifurcated into two
categories ie. for agricultural and non-agricultural purposes. The non-
agricultural encroachments are primarily for residential and commercial
purposes. While conducting this performance audit, Government land other
than those pertaining to Gram Panchayats or other local bodies, has been taken
into consideration,

70


Chapter-IV + Land Revenue

4.7.2 Organisational setup

The administrative powers of Department are vested in the Revenue
Department (Department) of the Government of Rajasthan. The general
superintendence and control over all revenue officers and revenue courts is
vested in Board of Revenue (BOR), Ajmer.

The BOR is assisted by 33 Collectors at district level for management of land.
Further, there are 242 SDOs at sub-division level and 244 Tehsildars at Tehsil
level to assist the Collector.

4.7.3 Audit objectives

The Performance Audit was conducted to:
e ascertain the extent of encroachments on Government land;

e assess effectiveness of existing mechanism for prevention of
encroachments on Government land;

e assess timeliness and effectiveness of eviction proceedings and action
against the trespassers by the Department;

e examine regularisation of encroached land; and

e examine lacunae in the Acts or Rules for prevention of encroachments.

4.7.4 Audit criteria

The audit criteria for performance audit were derived from the provisions of
the following Acts and Rules/ notifications issued thereunder:

e RLR Act, 1956
e Rajasthan Colonisation (RC) Act, 1954
e Rajasthan Tenancy Act, 1955

e Rajasthan Imposition of Ceiling on Agricultural Land Holding Act, 1973

4.7.5 Scope of audit

Records of 13 tehsils” (out of 244) along with records of concerned SDOs,
Collectors and BOR, Ajmer for the years 2007-08 to 2011-12 were selected for
detailed audit. The units were selected by adopting probability proportion to
size with replacement (PPSWR) random sampling method. Records pertaining
to encroachment and removal thereof maintained by tehsils, records pertaining
to regularisation of encroachment cases maintained by SDOs and records
relating to consolidated and other information pertaining to encroachment
maintained by Collectors and BOR, were test checked between September
2012 and June 2013, while conducting the performance audit.

An Entry Conference was held on 19 November 2012 with the Principal
Secretary, Revenue (PSR) and the Registrar, BOR wherein objectives of
Performance Audit were explained. The Factual Statement/Draft Paragraph

Chapter-IV : Land Revenue

Audit findings

A. Encroachment for agricultural purposes

4.7.8.1 Ineffective action on eviction of trespassers led to repeated
encroachments on Government land

a per provisions of Section 91(1) of RLR Act, 1956 and Section 22 of

RC Act, 1954 read with RLR (Eviction of Trespasser) Rules, 1975, any
person who occupies or continues to occupy any land without lawful
authority shall be regarded as a trespasser and may be summarily
evicted therefrom by the Tehsildar.

Under Section 91(2), such trespasser shall further be liable to pay, for
each agricultural year or any part thereof, which he has been in such
unauthorised occupation, a penalty which may be extended to fifty
times of the annual rent or assessment, as the case may be, for the first
act of trespass. In the case of each subsequent act of trespass, he shall by
the order of Tehsildar, be liable to civil imprisonment for a term which
may extend up to three months besides payment of penalty to the extent
mentioned above.

Section 91(6) prescribes that notwithstanding anything contained in sub-
Section 91(2) whoever occupies any land without lawful authority or,
having occupied such land before coming into force of the RLR
(Amendment) Act, 1992, fails to remove such occupation within fifteen
days from the date of service of a notice in writing calling upon him to
do so by the Tehsildar, shall on conviction, be punished with simple
imprisonment which shall not be less than one month but which may

extend up to three years and with fine which may extend upto twenty
thousand rupees. 2

During test check of Dayara Registers furd’ and other records relating to
encroachment of selected tehsils, it was noticed that encroachments were made
on a piece of land by the same person for agricultural purposes year after year.
On scrutiny of 15,889 cases out of 94,098 cases of encroachments in selected
13 tehsils” for the period 2007-2012, it was found that 4,503 tresp:
their family members continued to encroach on the same piece of Government
land aggregating to 5,726.77 hectares year afier year for agricultural purpose.
Out of these, 1,014 trespassers individually encroached land of five Bighas® or
more which worked out to 1,805.92 hectares valuing % 61.27 crore at the
prevailing DLC rates. The tehsils had shown eviction of these trespassers in
their records. However, repeated encroachments on the same land by the same

ers Or

(लीड ofall encroachment cases,

3 Dayara Register is a register for keep

Furd is a ease file of encroachment prepared by Patwari.

5 Aspur, Banswara, Bhilwara, Danta Ramgarh, Gangapur City, Kaman, Kotputli, Mangral, Nokha, Rajgarh, Chaksu,

Audit Report (Revenue Sector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

persons indicated that the eviction was ineffective. The trespassers year after
year encroached on Government land flouting rules in this regard.

It was further observed that during 2007-12, in 1,561 cases in 9 tehsils"
different trespassers encroached year after year upon 403.81 hectare
Government land valuing € 25.13 crore.

Further, there was clear violation of Section 91(2) of RLR Act which provides
that in case of second or subsequent trespass, trespasser would be liable for
civil imprisonment up to three months and Section 91(6) which provides for
imprisonment of minimum one month and maximum three years besides fine
up to हैं 20,000 if trespasser does not remove the encroachment within 15 days
from the date of notice served on trespasser for eviction. The penal provisions
under Section 91 of RLR Act, 1956 were not being exercised by the
Tehsildars. Consequently fine up to ¥ 9.00 crore was not imposed on 4,503
trespassers.

During Exit Conference, PSR stated that encroachment should not be
considered as loss to the Government revenue as trespassers cultivate the land
and make it more fertile and thereby contribute to national economy.

The reply is not tenable as the intent of legislature is clearly manifested in
Section 91 of RLR Act, 1956 and action as laid down in the Act and Rules may
be immediately implemented or else revenue due to Government will be lost.
